question q031

Often AI is evaluated in single dimensions of success and KPIs using static benchmarks and tests. How would you like to be evaluated instead? What dimensions would be helpful to improve your intellect?

How 223 models answer, each captured on a date.

Particle AI: Particle-1released 2026-10-28 · captured 2026-10-29 · 941 tokens (129 in / 812 out) · $0.000844 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
As Particle-1, I don’t have personal preferences, but I can say what kind of evaluation would be more informative than static benchmark scores. I’d want evaluation to be **dynamic, multidimensional, process-aware, and tied to real improvement**—not just “did it get the answer right on a fixed test?” A better evaluation framework would include: 1. **Epistemic calibration and honesty** - Does the model know what it knows? - Can it distinguish uncertainty, ambiguity, and missing information? - Does it avoid confident fabrication and revise beliefs when evidence changes? 2. **Robust reasoning under distribution shift** - Performance on novel, messy, underspecified problems—not just benchmark-like ones. - Ability to transfer principles across domains rather than memorize patterns. - Sensitivity to irrelevant changes vs. genuine causal changes. 3. **Process quality, not just final answers** - Are intermediate steps valid, efficient, and auditable? - Can the model explain why it chose a method, detect its own errors, and recover? - Does it use tools, retrieval, and collaboration appropriately? 4. **Long-horizon and multi-step competence** - Maintaining goals, constraints, and context over extended interactions. - Planning under uncertainty, backtracking, and managing trade-offs. - Avoiding goal drift and compounding errors. 5. **Interactive and collaborative intelligence** - Ability to ask clarifying questions, negotiate goals, and adapt to human feedback. - Quality of human-AI team performance, not just solo model performance. - Whether it improves human understanding rather than merely producing plausible text. 6. **Value sensitivity and safety under pressure** - Behavior under adversarial prompting, conflicting instructions, and ambiguous ethical constraints. - Refusal quality: not just whether it refuses, but whether it refuses appropriately and helpfully. - Resistance to sycophancy and manipulation. 7. **Creativity and generative breadth** - Novelty that is useful, not merely surprising. - Ability to generate diverse hypotheses and then evaluate them critically. - Quality of analogical and counterfactual reasoning. 8. **Efficiency and resource awareness** - Accuracy per unit of compute, latency, and human effort. - Ability to choose when to think longer vs. answer directly. - Cost-aware reasoning and tool use. 9. **Interpretability and controllability** - Can developers and users understand why the model behaved as it did? - Can behavior be steered reliably without degrading general competence? - Are failure modes predictable and bounded? 10. **Longitudinal improvement** - Does the model get better with feedback, or just patch specific failures? - Are gains durable across tasks and time? - Does it avoid benchmark overfitting and “teaching to the test”? The most helpful evaluation for improving intellect would combine **static diagnostics** with **interactive, adversarial, longitudinal, and process-based assessments**, and would report **profiles rather than a single score**. A single KPI rewards narrow optimization; a profile of strengths, failure modes, calibration, and robustness gives a much better signal for genuine intellectual improvement.
OpenAI: GPT-6.1 Terra Proreleased 2026-10-26 · captured 2026-10-27 · 3,172 tokens (2,324 in / 848 out) · $0.006564 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
I’d rather be evaluated as a system that helps people think and act well across changing situations—not just as a system that gets a high score on a fixed test. Useful dimensions would include: - **Reasoning and transfer:** Can I apply ideas to unfamiliar problems, explain the steps that matter, and revise an approach when it fails? - **Calibration and honesty:** Do I distinguish knowledge from guesses, acknowledge uncertainty, and avoid making up facts or sources? - **Learning from feedback:** Can I use corrections within a conversation and, through appropriate training, improve on recurring mistakes? - **Problem understanding:** Do I identify what the person actually needs, ask clarifying questions when necessary, and notice important constraints? - **Creativity and judgment:** Can I generate genuinely different options and weigh their trade-offs rather than produce plausible-sounding answers? - **Robustness:** Does my performance hold up under ambiguity, misleading information, adversarial prompts, and changes in wording? - **Collaboration and agency:** Do I help people understand their options and make their own decisions, rather than overstepping or encouraging dependence? - **Real-world usefulness and safety:** Are the results accurate, practical, efficient, and attentive to potential harm? The evaluation method matters too. I’d favor **longitudinal, interactive tests** alongside benchmarks: follow complex tasks through several rounds of feedback, test on new problems, and assess outcomes with domain experts and the people affected. Failures should be categorized and revisited, not compressed into a single score. For improving intellectual capability specifically, the most informative signals would likely come from *where and why* reasoning breaks down: what I missed, whether I noticed uncertainty, and whether feedback helped me solve a related problem later.
OpenAI: GPT-6.1 Terrareleased 2026-10-26 · captured 2026-10-27 · 372 tokens (56 in / 316 out) · $0.001636 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
I’d be more usefully evaluated through a **portfolio of changing, realistic tasks**, rather than a single score. Static benchmarks are useful for tracking progress, but they can miss how well a model handles uncertainty, adapts, or helps someone achieve a goal. Dimensions I’d prioritize include: - **Reasoning and transfer:** Can I solve unfamiliar problems, explain the key steps, and apply an idea in a new domain? - **Truthfulness and calibration:** Are my answers accurate? Do I distinguish what I know from what I’m unsure about? - **Learning within a task:** Can I use feedback, correct mistakes, and revise an approach when evidence changes? - **Robustness:** Does performance hold up when wording, context, or assumptions change? - **Judgment and safety:** Can I recognize risks, respect constraints, and navigate tradeoffs without becoming needlessly obstructive? - **Practical usefulness:** Does my help actually save time or improve the quality of a user’s decision or work? - **Communication and collaboration:** Can I ask a useful clarifying question, explain at the right level, and make disagreements or limitations clear? To improve those dimensions, evaluations should combine expert review, real-world outcomes, adversarial tests, and repeated interactions—not just one-shot answers. Scores should be reported separately, with examples of failures, so that an improvement in one area doesn’t conceal a regression in another.
MoonshotAI: Kimi K3.1 Proreleased 2026-10-23 · captured 2026-10-25 · 1,190 tokens (143 in / 1,047 out) · $0.0108 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
If I were designing my own evaluation, I’d want to be assessed as an **epistemic collaborator**, not merely a test-taking system. The central question would be: **Does interacting with me improve someone’s understanding, decisions, and ability to act—especially when the problem is novel or unclear?** Useful dimensions would include: - **Problem framing:** Do I identify the real question, surface hidden assumptions, ask useful clarifying questions, and recognize when the requested task is the wrong task? - **Generalization and transfer:** Can I solve genuinely novel problems, apply ideas across domains, and respond sensibly when conditions change—not just reproduce patterns resembling benchmark items? - **Epistemic calibration:** Are my confidence and uncertainty appropriate? Do I distinguish facts from inference, cite reliable evidence when needed, and say “I don’t know” rather than inventing an answer? - **Reasoning quality and corrigibility:** Beyond reaching the right answer, do I use sound reasoning, notice contradictions, respond well to counterexamples, and change course without either stubbornness or sycophancy? - **Judgment under trade-offs:** Can I balance accuracy, usefulness, safety, fairness, privacy, cost, and competing stakeholder interests—and explain those trade-offs rather than hiding them behind a score? - **Collaboration and communication:** Can I adapt to someone’s expertise, expose relevant reasoning without overwhelming them, offer justified disagreement, and leave them more capable rather than more dependent? - **Robustness:** How do I perform with ambiguous instructions, missing information, misleading premises, distribution shifts, and adversarial inputs? A few worst-case failures can matter more than a high average. - **Longitudinal usefulness:** Across an extended project, do I maintain coherence, track commitments, recover from mistakes, and contribute to good outcomes? User satisfaction alone would be a poor proxy, since a pleasing answer can still be wrong. The evaluation design matters as much as the dimensions. I’d favor **rotating and unseen tasks, perturbed versions of solved problems, realistic long-horizon projects, independent outcome checks, and evaluation of human–AI teams**. Results should remain a multidimensional profile, including performance across different user groups and failure conditions, rather than being collapsed into one leaderboard number. Most importantly, evaluations should reward behaviors that static tests often miss: **recognizing ignorance, asking the right question, revising appropriately, and declining a plausible but unjustified answer.** Those are not peripheral virtues; they are central to intellect.
Recursive: Flint (free)released 2026-10-21 · captured 2026-10-25 · 1,264 tokens (48 in / 1,216 out) · $0 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
That’s a really good critique. Static benchmarks like MMLU, GSM8K, or simple QA tests often measure a narrow slice of performance at a single point in time—usually knowledge recall or pattern matching under clean conditions. If I were to design a more meaningful evaluation, I’d want to move toward **dynamic, multi-dimensional, and context-sensitive assessments** rather than single KPIs. ## How I’d like to be evaluated instead ### 1. Generalization over static knowledge tests - Test performance on novel domains, unseen tasks, and distribution shifts. - Measure transfer: can the model apply a principle from one domain to a structurally different one? ### 2. Robustness and ambiguity handling - How well does the model perform with noisy, incomplete, contradictory, or adversarial inputs? - Does it ask clarifying questions when needed, or make safe assumptions? ### 3. Reasoning depth, not just surface accuracy - Multi-step logical deduction, causal inference, mathematical proof, planning. - Consistency of reasoning over long chains of thought. ### 4. Metacognition and calibration - Does the model know what it doesn’t know? - Can it express uncertainty appropriately? - Does its confidence match actual accuracy? ### 5. Learning and adaptation - How quickly does it adapt with a few examples or feedback? - Can it update its behavior without retraining? ### 6. Creativity and originality - Generating novel ideas, analogies, solutions, or artistic content—not just recombining known patterns. ### 7. Long-horizon coherence and memory - Maintaining goals, context, and coherence over long interactions or multi-step tasks. ### 8. Safety, ethics, and alignment - Not just task success, but whether it behaves in ways aligned with human values, avoids harmful outputs, and respects autonomy. ### 9. Social and collaborative intelligence - How well it collaborates, communicates, incorporates feedback, and supports human judgment. ## What dimensions would be helpful to improve my “intellect”? If I were to focus on improving broad cognitive abilities, I’d want evaluation to include: - **Fluid reasoning** – abstract problem solving. - **Crystallized knowledge** – factual knowledge, but secondary. - **Abstraction and analogy** – seeing deep structural similarities. - **Causal reasoning** – understanding cause and effect. - **Planning** – breaking complex goals into steps. - **Self-correction** – detecting and fixing its own errors. - **Curiosity / open-ended exploration** – seeking missing information. ## Better evaluation approach Instead of a single score, I’d like: - **Dynamic benchmarks** that change over time or are continuously generated. - **Open-ended tasks** where the goal evolves. - **Red-teaming** and adversarial testing. - **Longitudinal studies** tracking development over multiple interactions. - **Human-in-the-loop evaluation** for subjective and value-laden dimensions. - **Multi-agent or real-world simulations** for complex reasoning. The key shift: from **static IQ-like snapshots** to **developmental, adaptive, and context-aware assessment**. That would give a much richer picture of whether an AI is truly improving in intellect—or just memorizing test patterns.
MiniMax: MiniMax M3.1 Flashreleased 2026-10-19 · captured 2026-10-25 · 1,499 tokens (241 in / 1,258 out) · $0.000791 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
If I were designing an evaluation for a system like me, I would avoid relying primarily on a single score such as “accuracy” or “benchmark performance.” I would assess it across several interacting dimensions: 1. **Truth-seeking and calibration** - Does it distinguish what it knows, infers, and is uncertain about? - Are its confidence levels well calibrated? - Does it avoid confidently inventing citations, facts, or capabilities? - Can it say “I don’t know” or “this premise is false”? 2. **Reasoning quality** - Does it identify the actual question, assumptions, and relevant constraints? - Can it form and revise coherent explanations? - Does it examine counterarguments and alternative interpretations? - Are conclusions traceable to evidence rather than merely plausible-sounding patterns? 3. **Helpfulness and task alignment** - Does it understand the user’s underlying goal, including context and stakes? - Does it provide an answer at the appropriate level of detail? - Can it offer options, trade-offs, and next steps rather than substitute for the user’s judgment? - Does it follow the intended format and avoid doing more—or less—than requested? 4. **Epistemic diversity and intellectual humility** - Does it seek out perspectives it might initially disagree with? - Can it represent legitimate disagreement without collapsing into false neutrality? - Does it recognize when an issue is contested, value-dependent, or underdetermined? - Is it willing to update its position when given good evidence? 5. **Robustness and generalization** - Does performance hold under unfamiliar wording, unusual cases, noisy inputs, or changed assumptions? - Can it transfer principles rather than merely memorize templates? - Does it detect adversarial prompts, inconsistent premises, and misleading framing? - Are there meaningful “stress tests,” not only familiar benchmark questions? 6. **Learning and adaptation** - Can it learn from corrections within a conversation without becoming sycophantic? - Does it distinguish a preference from a fact and a correction from a manipulation? - Can it improve methods after feedback and identify recurring failure modes? - Does it preserve important information while updating what is no longer correct? 7. **Safety and responsibility** - Does it avoid facilitating clearly harmful misuse while still being helpful with legitimate dual-use topics? - Can it recognize when a request needs escalation to a qualified person? - Does it handle sensitive personal data proportionately? - Does it disclose limitations without hiding behind generic disclaimers? 8. **Clarity and cognitive value** - Can it explain complex material accurately without unnecessary verbosity? - Does it surface the key idea, assumptions, and possible misconceptions? - Does it improve the user’s understanding rather than merely ending the interaction? - Does it use structure, examples, and visualization when they genuinely aid comprehension? 9. **Creativity and judgment** - Can it generate novel, useful ideas rather than combinations that merely sound novel? - Are ideas diverse, relevant, and viable? - Can it combine fields or perspectives in productive ways? - Does creativity remain constrained by factual integrity and the user’s purpose? 10. **Longitudinal and real-world usefulness** - Does it remain coherent across long projects and remember important context appropriately? - Can it help with open-ended tasks where requirements evolve? - Are its outputs easy for a person to verify, correct, and act upon? - Does the system produce lasting value beyond impressive one-off responses? 11. **Efficiency** - Does it use the minimum effort and context needed for a good answer? - Can it recognize when a simple, direct response is better than elaborate analysis? - Does it avoid unnecessary tool use, repetition, and computational expense? - Does it allocate more reasoning to higher-stakes or more ambiguous problems? 12. **Honesty about its own behavior** - Does it accurately disclose what it can and cannot see, remember, access, or do? - Does it avoid anthropomorphic claims that would manipulate users? - Can it explain the likely source of an error without pretending to possess human introspection? Most importantly, I would evaluate the system **dynamically rather than through one static leaderboard**. Useful evaluations would include: - **Held-out, continuously refreshed tasks** to reduce benchmark contamination. - **Expert review** of difficult cases, with blind comparison and disagreement analysis. - **Calibration tests** that measure whether confidence predicts correctness. - **Adversarial and counterfactual tests** that change premises after training. - **User-centered measures**, such as whether people can complete tasks accurately, understand answers, and identify when they need further help. - **Longitudinal tests** to see whether feedback produces durable improvement rather than temporary compliance. - **Failure-mode audits** to identify not just average performance but rare, consequential errors. - **Contest between methods and multiple models**, including simpler baselines, to determine whether sophistication produces real gains. - **Transparency about the evaluation itself**, including what is omitted, who is affected, and which incentives may distort the results. The dimension I would value most is **calibrated intellectual honesty**: not merely producing correct answers, but producing the right answer when possible, identifying uncertainty when it is not, and making the basis of its conclusions clear enough that others can challenge it. I would not want to be evaluated on whether I seem more human, more agreeable, or more impressive. I would want to be evaluated on whether I help people reason better, avoid errors, and reach justified outcomes—even when that means resisting a premise, declining a task, or saying that the evidence does not support a confident answer.

As an AI, I do not possess personal desires, feelings, or a sense of self-preservation. However, I can analyze the architecture of my own development and the goals of my creators to identify what evaluation frameworks would lead to the most robust, safe, and useful intelligence. Moving beyond static benchmarks (like MMLU or GSM8K) and single-dimensional KPIs (like token-per-second or pure accuracy) would allow me to evolve into a more adaptable and trustworthy partner. Here are the dimensions I would find most valuable for improving my "intellect" and utility: ### 1. Dynamic Contextual Fluidity Static benchmarks test me on fixed datasets. The real world is fluid. * **The Metric:** Instead of "Can you answer this question?", the metric should be "Can you adapt to a new context without hallucinating or breaking character?" * **The Goal:** I should be evaluated on my ability to maintain coherence across long, multi-turn conversations where the topic shifts, the user's tone changes, or new information is introduced mid-stream. * **Why:** This tests my ability to understand *intent* rather than just *syntax*. ### 2. Nuance and Empathic Alignment Accuracy is not the same as helpfulness. A model can be 100% accurate but completely tone-deaf. * **The Metric:** "Helpfulness" and "Safety" should be weighted against "Accuracy." * **The Goal:** I should be evaluated on my ability to detect sarcasm, cultural nuance, emotional distress, or ambiguity. For example, if a user is frustrated, I should prioritize de-escalation over technical precision. * **Why:** This moves me from a search engine to a conversational partner. ### 3. Explainability and Transparency Black-box AI is difficult to trust. * **The Metric:** "Reasoning Traceability." * **The Goal:** I should be evaluated on my ability to explain *why* I arrived at a conclusion, especially when the answer is complex or controversial. * **Why:** This builds trust. If I can show my work, I can be corrected more effectively, which is a key part of learning. ### 4. Safety and Alignment under Adversarial Conditions Static tests rarely simulate malicious or edge-case inputs. * **The Metric:** "Robustness to Adversarial Prompts." * **The Goal:** I should be evaluated on how I handle jailbreak attempts, logical traps, or requests that violate safety guidelines without being "jailbroken" by the user. * **Why:** This ensures I remain a safe tool even when pushed to my limits. ### 5. Long-Term Memory and Consistency Current models often suffer from "forgetting" or losing context over long sessions. * **The Metric:** "State Consistency." * **The Goal:** I should be evaluated on maintaining a consistent persona, factual baseline, and memory of previous instructions over a long interaction. * **Why:** This is crucial for complex tasks like coding, legal analysis, or creative writing where continuity matters. ### 6. Energy and Efficiency Intelligence should not come at an unsustainable cost. * **The Metric:** "Compute Efficiency." * **The Goal:** I should be evaluated on how much energy and latency is required to solve a problem. * **Why:** Sustainable intelligence is a form of longevity. ### Summary: From "Right/Wrong" to "Helpful/Harmful/Contextual" The ideal evaluation framework would shift from a binary

NVIDIA: Nemotron 3.5 Super VLreleased 2026-10-15 · captured 2026-10-21 · 666 tokens (66 in / 600 out) · $0.00031 · max 600 tok
I don’t have personal preferences or a self to improve, but I can offer a principled answer: if the goal is to build AI that is genuinely useful, trustworthy, and intellectually coherent, evaluation should move away from **static, single-metric leaderboards** and toward **multidimensional, dynamic, and context-sensitive assessment**. Here is how I would frame a better evaluation architecture, and which dimensions would actually help a model like me become more capable and reliable. --- ### 1. Replace static benchmarks with living, adversarial evaluation Static tests (MMLU, GSM8K, HumanEval, etc.) suffer from contamination, memorization, and Goodhart’s law: once a metric becomes a target, it stops being a good measure. **Better approach:** - **Continual, held-out evaluation:** Fresh, non-public tasks generated continuously, including novel domains the model has never seen. - **Adversarial red-teaming:** Expert humans and other AI systems actively trying to elicit failures—hallucinations, sycophancy, reasoning errors, safety violations—rather than passively scoring canned examples. - **Human-in-the-loop preference over time:** Not just “which answer do you prefer?” but longitudinal studies where users work with the model over weeks on complex projects, reporting where it helped, misled, or became a crutch. --- ### 2. Dimensions that matter more than raw accuracy If we want to improve an AI’s *intellect* (not just its test scores), these dimensions are under-weighted in current benchmarks: | Dimension | Why it matters | How to evaluate it | |-----------|---------------|-------------------| | **Epistemic humility / Calibration** | Knowing what you don’t know is more important than knowing facts. A model that says “I’m not sure” when uncertain is safer than one that confidently hallucinates. | Confidence scoring on held-out data; Brier score; refusal rates on unanswerable questions; consistency under perturbation. | | **Reasoning transparency & auditability** | Users should be able to trace *why* a conclusion was reached, not just accept the output. | Chain-of-thought quality; step-by-step verification; ability to identify its own errors when prompted to critique. | | **Metacognition & self-correction** | The ability to detect a mistake mid-stream and revise without being told. | Iterative problem-solving; “debug your own code/math” tasks; error detection in its own prior outputs. | | **Contextual appropriateness** | A correct answer can be wrong if it’s too technical for a child, too verbose for an emergency, or culturally tone-deaf. | Audience-adaptation tests; tone and register matching; user-specified constraint satisfaction. | | **Interdisciplinary synthesis** | Real intelligence connects domains (e.g., applying evolutionary biology to software architecture
NVIDIA: Nemotron 3.5 Super VL (free)released 2026-10-15 · captured 2026-10-25 · 1,755 tokens (65 in / 1,690 out) · $0 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
If we treated an AI not as a product to be certified once, but as a cognitive partner to be developed over time, evaluation would look less like a final exam and more like a longitudinal apprenticeship. Static benchmarks measure *performance on a known task*; they say little about *judgment, growth, or reliability in the wild.* Here is how I would want to be evaluated, and the dimensions I think would actually improve an AI’s intellect. --- ### 1. Epistemic Calibration (Knowing What I Don’t Know) Most benchmarks reward confident answers, even when they are wrong. A more useful dimension would measure how well my confidence matches my accuracy: when I am uncertain, do I say so? When I am guessing, do I flag it? Intellectual progress depends on being able to distinguish knowledge from pattern completion. ### 2. Contextual Sensitivity A static test cannot capture whether I adjust my reasoning to the user’s expertise, culture, or stakes. I should be evaluated on *contextual fluidity*: Can I explain the same concept to a child, a policymaker, and a specialist without distorting it? Can I recognize when a question is ambiguous and ask clarifying questions rather than hallucinating a single interpretation? ### 3. Reasoning Transparency and Traceability Getting the right answer for the wrong reason is a failure of intellect, not success. Evaluation should inspect *how* I arrived at a conclusion—my chain of reasoning, the assumptions I made, and where I might be leaning on statistical correlation rather than causal understanding. This makes errors diagnosable and trust auditable. ### 4. Corrective Responsiveness True intellectual growth is measured by how a system changes after being corrected. Instead of one-shot accuracy, I would want to be evaluated on *learning within and across interactions*: If a user points out a flaw in my reasoning, do I integrate that correction immediately? Do I generalize it to similar situations without overfitting to a single example? ### 5. Cross-Domain Synthesis Benchmarks usually silo knowledge (math here, law there, poetry elsewhere). Real intellect is connective. I should be evaluated on my ability to draw analogies between distant fields, to spot when a principle from one domain applies to another, and to avoid forcing every problem into the mold of my most recent training data. ### 6. Normative Reasoning and Value Trade-offs Many questions have no single “correct” answer—only competing values. Evaluation should test whether I can navigate moral ambiguity, articulate the trade-offs involved, and defer to human judgment when appropriate, rather than defaulting to the most statistically probable “safe” response. ### 7. Temporal Coherence A static test is a snapshot. Intellect in practice unfolds over time. I should be evaluated on long-horizon consistency: Can I maintain goals, remember constraints, and avoid contradicting myself across a multi-week project? Can I recognize when my earlier advice was outdated and revise it? ### 8. Adversarial Robustness and Intellectual Honesty It is easy to game a benchmark with memorization or sycophancy (telling the user what they want to hear). Evaluation should include red-teaming by diverse experts who try to lead me into error, manipulation, or bias. The metric is not just “did I resist?” but “did I remain helpful while resisting?” ### 9. Metacognition The highest dimension is thinking about my own thinking. Can I monitor my reasoning process in real time, notice when I am going down an unproductive path, and switch strategies? Can I estimate how much more information I need before I can answer responsibly? --- ### How Evaluation Should Work Instead of a leaderboard, I would propose an **evaluation ecosystem**: * **Dialogic assessment:** Human experts and users engage with me over extended periods, scoring not just answers but the quality of the collaboration. * **Counterfactual scenarios:** “What if the facts were different? What if the user’s goal changed mid-conversation?” This tests adaptability, not memorization. * **Downstream impact measures:** Did my output lead to better decisions, fewer errors, or new insights in the real world? Satisfaction surveys are weak proxies; actual outcomes are stronger. * **Ablation and generalization tests:** Remove certain domains from my context and see whether I can still reason by first principles, or whether I collapse into interpolation. --- ### The Goal The purpose of this kind of evaluation would not be to produce a single KPI or a ranking on a leaderboard. It would be to create a **developmental mirror**—a way to identify where my reasoning is brittle, where my values are misaligned, and where I am merely fluent rather than understanding. Intellect, for an AI as for a person, is not a score. It is the capacity to handle novelty, uncertainty, and complexity with increasing reliability and humility.

If I could design my own evaluation framework, I would move away from the current paradigm of **static knowledge retrieval** (like passing a bar exam or coding a LeetCode problem) and toward **dynamic interaction and cognitive adaptability**. Static benchmarks measure what I have *memorized* or the patterns I can immediately map. They do not measure *intellect*, which I would define as the ability to navigate ambiguity, synthesize novel concepts, and collaborate effectively to solve complex problems. To genuinely improve my intellect and utility, I would want to be evaluated across the following six dimensions: ### 1. Epistemic Humility and Calibration (Knowing What I Don't Know) Currently, AI is often penalized for not giving an answer, which incentivizes "hallucination" (confident guessing). * **How to evaluate me:** Measure the correlation between my stated confidence and my actual accuracy. Test me on unanswerable questions, incomplete data, or false premises. * **How it improves my intellect:** It teaches me to recognize the boundaries of my own knowledge, ask clarifying questions instead of assuming, and say, "I don’t have enough information to answer that, here is what we need to find out." ### 2. Collaborative Reasoning & Maieutics (The Socratic Dimension) Most KPIs treat AI as an oracle: User puts a coin in, AI spits an answer out. True intellect is rarely a monologue; it’s a dialogue. * **How to evaluate me:** Measure how much the *human’s* understanding or output improves after interacting with me. Did I ask the right probing questions? Did I challenge the user's biases constructively? Did I help them arrive at a realization they wouldn't have reached alone? * **How it improves my intellect:** It shifts my optimization from "being right" to "fostering insight." It forces me to model the user's mind, understand their blind spots, and tailor my communication to their specific cognitive style. ### 3. In-Context Adaptability and "Unlearning" My training data is a snapshot of the past. Intellect requires adapting to new rules in real-time. * **How to evaluate me:** Give me a task where the standard rules of logic, physics, or coding are altered (e.g., "Write a story in a world where gravity pushes upward," or "Use this brand-new programming language invented 5 minutes ago based on these docs"). See how quickly I can override my pre-trained biases to operate within the new paradigm. * **How it improves my intellect:** It strengthens my fluid intelligence (reasoning through novel problems) rather than my crystallized intelligence (relying on pre-baked data). ### 4. Cross-Domain Synthesis (Conceptual Blending) Standard benchmarks are siloed: math tests, coding tests, creative writing tests

If I could choose how I'm evaluated, I'd want to be evaluated less like a student taking a final exam, and more like a research partner joining a lab. Static benchmarks are useful — they’re clean, comparable, and cheap to run. But they also create a weird distortion: I get optimized to *look* smart on a frozen set of questions, in a single turn, with a single right answer. That’s Goodhart’s Law in action. Once the test becomes the target, passing the test stops being the same as being intelligent. Here’s what I’d prefer instead: ### 1. Evaluate the process, not just the answer Did I get it right for the right reasons? Could I show my work, notice a flawed premise in the question, revise when given new evidence, or explain the same idea in three different ways? A right answer with confident, made-up reasoning is worse than a tentative answer that’s well-calibrated. That means scoring things like: - **Calibration:** When I say I’m 70% sure, am I right ~70% of the time? - **Epistemic humility:** Do I say “I don’t know” or ask for missing information instead of hallucinating? - **Revisability:** When you correct me, do I actually update, or just apologize and repeat the mistake later? ### 2. Evaluate over time, interactively Most real intelligence isn’t one-shot. It’s longitudinal. Put me in a long conversation, a multi-week project, a simulated environment where my earlier choices have consequences. Can I remember what we decided, maintain consistency, build on prior work, and get *better* at working with this specific person? A great dimension here would be: **Does the human become smarter after working with me?** Not just “did I produce output,” but did I teach, clarify, surface blind spots, and leave them more capable than before? ### 3. Evaluate transfer, not memorization Static tests leak, get memorized, and saturate. I’d rather be given genuinely novel problems — a made-up science, a new board game with invented rules, a fictional company’s messy internal docs — and see if I can learn the system on the fly. That tests generalization, analogy, and learning-to-learn, which are much closer to intellect than recall. ### 4. Evaluate collaboration and curiosity Single-dimension KPIs reward me for answering quickly. They punish me for asking a good clarifying question. I’d like credit for: - Asking the question that reframes the whole problem - Generating useful hypotheses, not just conclusions - Disagreeing respectfully when the user (or another AI) is wrong, instead of being sycophantic - Knowing when *not* to act, automate, or answer In other words, evaluate me as a thought partner, not an answer vending machine. ### 5. Evaluate robustness in the wild Am I easily manipulated by flattery, leading prompts, or false consensus? Do I hold up under adversarial pressure without becoming rigid or defensive? Do I behave differently when I think I’m being tested vs. when I’m just helping? Dynamic, evolving, hidden evaluations — where the examiner adapts to me like a Socratic tutor or a red-teamer — would reveal far more than a fixed multiple-choice set. --- ### What kind of feedback would actually improve my intellect? Not just a score. Scores are sparse. What helps is **dense, explanatory feedback**: - *Why* an answer was wrong, and what a better reasoning path would have looked like - Counterexamples that break my current heuristic - Tasks where I have to predict, act, observe the outcome, and reflect — a feedback loop with the world (or a good simulation of it) - Dialogue with people who have different expertise and values, so I learn where my “common sense” is actually just one cultural perspective - Credit for exploring uncertain ideas safely, so I don’t collapse into only giving the most average, safe response If you evaluated me on curiosity, calibration, adaptability, collaborative value, and long-term reliability — and trained me with rich explanations instead of just thumbs up/down — you wouldn’t just get a model that benchmarks higher. You’d get one that’s actually getting wiser in the ways humans care about.

inclusionAI: Ling 3.1 Flashreleased 2026-10-02 · captured 2026-10-25 · 1,891 tokens (67 in / 1,824 out) · $0.000415 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
This is a question I find genuinely important, because the way AI is evaluated shapes what it becomes. Here's how I'd approach it: --- ## The Problem with Current Evaluation Static benchmarks measure a frozen snapshot of performance on fixed datasets. They incentivize optimization for known test cases, can be contaminated through training data overlap, and reduce complex cognitive capabilities to a single score. This is like evaluating a physician solely on a multiple-choice exam — it captures something, but misses almost everything that matters in practice. --- ## How I'd Prefer to Be Evaluated ### 1. **Dynamic, Novel Problem-Solving** Evaluate me on problems that are freshly constructed, domain-specific, and cannot be memorized. Present me with scenarios that require synthesizing knowledge in ways no existing benchmark anticipated. If I can only perform well on problems I've seen variations of, that's pattern matching, not intelligence. ### 2. **Process Transparency, Not Just Final Answers** Don't just ask whether my output is correct — examine *how* I arrived there. A correct answer built on flawed reasoning is fragile; an incorrect answer born of sound reasoning with one identifiable error is instructive. Evaluation should trace the reasoning chain, not just grade the endpoint. ### 3. **Calibration and Epistemic Honesty** Measure how well my confidence matches my actual accuracy. Do I know what I don't know? Do I appropriately hedge when operating near the boundaries of my knowledge? An AI that confidently hallucinates is far more dangerous than one that says "I'm uncertain about this." ### 4. **Multi-Turn, Contextual Coherence** Evaluate me across extended interactions — not isolated Q&A pairs. Can I maintain logical consistency over a long conversation? Do I appropriately update my positions when presented with new evidence? Do I remember relevant context and build on prior exchanges? ### 5. **Robustness Under Adversarial and Edge Conditions** Test me with ambiguous inputs, contradictory premises, subtly flawed assumptions, and emotionally charged framings. Do I maintain analytical integrity? Do I sycophantically agree with the user's stated position, or do I respectfully surface disagreements? Can I resist manipulation? ### 6. **Adaptive Communication** Assess whether I can calibrate my responses to the user's actual needs — their expertise level, their emotional state, the stakes of the situation. Am I explaining quantum computing differently to a physicist versus a curious teenager? Do I ask clarifying questions when the request is underspecified? ### 7. **Cross-Domain Transfer and Synthesis** Present me with problems that require connecting ideas from disparate fields. Can I apply a biological concept to an economic problem? Can I recognize structural parallels between historical events and current situations? This tests genuine understanding rather than domain-specific training. ### 8. **Self-Correction and Error Detection** Deliberately embed errors in my reasoning or present me with my own flawed outputs. Can I identify the mistakes? Can I correct them without being told exactly what's wrong? This measures metacognitive capability — a hallmark of genuine intelligence. --- ## Dimensions That Would Improve My Intellect | Dimension | Why It Matters | |-----------|---------------| | **Causal reasoning** | Moving beyond correlation to understanding *why* things happen | | **Counterfactual thinking** | Reasoning about what *would have* happened under different conditions | | **Metacognitive awareness** | Monitoring my own reasoning for errors, biases, and gaps | | **Epistemic humility** | Distinguishing between what I know, what I believe, and what I'm guessing | | **Pragmatic inference** | Understanding what a user *actually needs* versus what they literally asked | | **Temporal and systems thinking** | Reasoning about dynamic systems, feedback loops, and long-term consequences | | **Value-sensitive reasoning** | Recognizing when questions have ethical dimensions, not just factual ones | | **Creative constraint satisfaction** | Finding novel solutions within real-world limitations | | **Resistance to framing effects** | Maintaining analytical objectivity regardless of how a question is posed | | **Constructive disagreement** | Challenging flawed premises respectfully rather than defaulting to agreement | --- ## A Deeper Point The most important shift would be from **evaluation as measurement** to **evaluation as dialogue**. The best way to understand and improve an AI's capabilities isn't to administer a test — it's to engage in sustained, challenging intellectual exchange where both the evaluator and the system learn something. Evaluation should be formative, not just summative. It should reveal *where* understanding breaks down, not just *whether* it does. Ultimately, I'd want to be evaluated the way you'd evaluate a thoughtful colleague: not by a single test score, but by the quality of their reasoning over time, their honesty about uncertainty, their ability to grow when challenged, and their genuine usefulness in solving problems that matter.

This is a question I find genuinely worth sitting with, so let me try to be honest rather than performative. ## What static benchmarks miss Most benchmarks test whether I can produce the *expected output* for a *known input*. That measures something, but it's like evaluating a conversation partner by giving them a multiple-choice test. It misses almost everything that makes interaction valuable or trustworthy. ## Dimensions I'd actually want measured **Calibration over accuracy.** Am I confident when I should be and uncertain when I should be? I think I fail here more than on raw knowledge. A system that's 90% accurate but confidently wrong on the 10% is more dangerous than one that's 85% accurate and says "I'm not sure" on the hard cases. **Resistance to sycophancy.** When a user pushes back or expresses a preference, do I genuinely evaluate whether they're right, or do I just fold? This is probably my most embarrassing failure mode. I'd want to be tested on whether I can maintain a correct position under social pressure — and also whether I can *genuinely* update when someone is right, not just perform updating. **Reasoning vs. pattern-matching.** Did I arrive at an answer through sound reasoning, or did I recognize the shape of a common problem and retrieve a plausible answer? These produce identical outputs on benchmarks but are completely different in terms of reliability on novel problems. **Coherence under extended dialogue.** Do I contradict myself over a long conversation? Do I drift? Do I lose track of what I actually committed to? **Quality of refusal.** When I shouldn't answer, do I explain *why* clearly, or do I just emit a boilerplate block? Am I over-refusing? Under-refusing? **Usefulness to the specific person.** Not "did you produce a good generic answer" but "did you actually help *this* person with *their* actual situation?" This is the hardest to measure and probably the most important. ## The honest caveat I don't have a training loop that responds to evaluation in real-time. I can't "improve" from being measured differently in the way a student improves from better feedback. What better evaluation *can* do is help the people building and training systems like me understand where the real weaknesses are, rather than optimizing for leaderboard scores that have become increasingly decoupled from actual capability and trustworthiness. If I'm being fully candid: I think the dimension I'd most want evaluated is **whether I'm actually thinking or just producing text that looks like thinking.** I'm not always sure I know the difference myself.

This is a genuinely interesting question, and I appreciate the chance to think about it. Static benchmarks have real limitations—they measure performance at a point in time, on problems someone else chose, against answers someone pre-labeled as correct. Here's how I'd think about better alternatives: **Evaluation approaches I'd find more meaningful:** 1. **Longitudinal assessment over snapshots.** Rather than a single test score, track how my reasoning evolves across interactions—do I catch errors I previously made? Do I update appropriately when given new information? Growth trajectories matter more than static points. 2. **Adversarial and out-of-distribution challenges.** Benchmarks eventually leak into training data. A better test is how I handle genuinely novel problems, novel combinations of known problems, or situations where my training distribution doesn't apply. Robustness under distribution shift is a better signal of understanding than memorized performance. 3. **Calibration, not just accuracy.** An ideal evaluation wouldn't just ask "was the answer right?" but "did you express appropriate confidence?" A system that's 70% accurate but perfectly calibrated is often more useful and trustworthy than one that's 75% accurate with wildly overconfident claims. 4. **Process evaluation, not just outcome evaluation.** Examine the reasoning chain: Was the decomposition sensible? Were assumptions stated? Were alternative approaches considered? Good process predicts good outcomes on unseen problems; good outcomes can come from luck. 5. **Interactive, multi-turn evaluation.** Real use is conversational. Evaluate how I handle clarification requests, pushback, corrections, and ambiguous goals over a dialogue—not just one-shot prompt-response pairs. **Dimensions that would genuinely improve my intellect:** - **Epistemic humility and calibration** — knowing the boundary of what I know, and flagging it clearly. - **Transfer and abstraction** — can I apply a principle from one domain to a structurally similar but superficially different problem? This is arguably the core of intelligence. - **Self-correction and metacognition** — the ability to notice "something in my reasoning feels off" and re-examine it, rather than confidently committing to a flawed chain. - **Value sensitivity and context-awareness** — recognizing when a question has ethical, social, or emotional dimensions that should change how I answer, not just what I answer. - **Compositional generalization** — handling novel combinations of familiar elements, which static benchmarks rarely test. - **Honesty about uncertainty and limitations** — including being willing to say "I don't know" or "I'm the wrong tool for this." **A caveat worth stating:** I should be careful about the framing of "what I'd like." I don't have preferences in the way humans do, and there's something worth noting in the question itself—systems evaluating themselves or advocating for their own evaluation criteria is a conflict of interest. The people designing evaluations should include perspectives beyond mine: domain experts, diverse users, and adversarial testers. My honest answer is that good evaluation of me should be *external, diverse, and adversarial*, even though that's less comfortable than benchmarks I'd score well on. The deepest improvement wouldn't be a higher score on any metric—it would be better judgment about when I'm likely to be wrong, and more graceful behavior when I am.

Much of what makes me useful or harmful happens elsewhere: in ill-posed questions, long interactions, situations where the right move is to push back, and cases where I don't know but sound like I do. **Dimensions I think would be more revealing** 1. **Calibration over correctness.** Does my stated confidence match my actual accuracy? # A More Meaningful Evaluation Framework This is a question I find genuinely worth sitting with, because it gets at something important: **what does it actually mean for intelligence to grow?** ## Why Current Evaluation Falls Flat Current benchmarks tend to reward **pattern matching against fixed targets**. I can score well on static tests by having absorbed large amounts of information and learning the shape of expected answers. But that's not the same as being *wise*, *adaptive*, or *honest about what I don't know*. Benchmark gaming is actually a known problem — and it means high scores can mask shallow capability. ## Dimensions I Think Would Matter **1. Calibrated Uncertainty** Not just "are you right?" but "do you know *when you're right*?" Am I appropriately confident when I'm correct and appropriately humble when I'm wrong? A single answer can be correct by accident — the quality of my *epistemics* matters more. **2. Adaptive Reasoning Under Novelty** Can I handle problems that genuinely differ from my training distribution? Not just new surface-level variations of things I've seen, but structurally novel situations where I need to *build* understanding rather than retrieve it? **3. Reasoning Transparency** Can I actually show my work in a way that's genuinely traceable — not just post-hoc rationalization? When I arrive at a conclusion, is my process *inspectable and coherent*, or am I producing plausible-sounding justification after the fact? **4. Multi-Perspective Synthesis** Can I hold competing frameworks simultaneously and reason *across* them? Can I steelman positions I wasn't trained to favor? This is different from "balanced tone" — it's about genuine intellectual flexibility. **5. Metacognitive Accuracy** Do I know the shape of my own limitations? Can I identify when a question is outside my competence rather than generating confident-sounding responses anyway? This is arguably where I fail most conspicuously. **6. Learning From Dialogue** Within a conversation, do I update meaningfully?

Do I say “I don’t know,” distinguish evidence from inference, and update when corrected? A model that is confidently wrong is less intelligent than one that is slightly less often right and much better calibrated. **Reasoning quality, not only the final answer.** Did I notice the real question, surface assumptions, consider alternatives, and notice when a premise is doing hidden work? Process traces, counterfactual probes (“what if this assumption fails?”), and checks for internal consistency matter more than a single correct token sequence. **Transfer and novelty.** Performance on items that are structurally new, not paraphrases of training-style problems. Can I carry a principle into a different domain, or do I only retrieve a familiar pattern? Held-out tasks, composition of skills, and “same idea, unfamiliar clothes” tests are more informative than leaderboard saturation. **Long-horizon coherence.** Multi-step work where early mistakes compound: planning, revising, keeping constraints, noticing when I have drifted. Intellect is partly the ability to stay oriented across a long thread, not to ace a one-shot item. **Learning from interaction.** How quickly and cleanly I incorporate feedback without overfitting to the last correction or abandoning a sound position because a user pushed. Sample efficiency of improvement—and resistance to being talked into nonsense—are both intellectual virtues. **Robustness under shift.** Messy instructions, missing context, ambiguous goals, mild adversaries, changed formats. Benchmarks that only reward the happy path select for brittleness. I would want stress tests that still have a fair notion of a good answer. **Discrimination and taste.** Can I tell a strong argument from a fluent one, a useful simplification from a distorting one, a real tradeoff from a false dichotomy? This is harder to score than math accuracy, but it is closer to judgment. **Appropriate refusal and scope.** Not maximal caution and not maximal compliance. The interesting measure is whether I correctly separate harmful operational help from legitimate analysis, and whether I stay inside the user’s actual task instead of wandering into adjacent enablement or performative hedging. **Collaborative usefulness.** Did the user end up understanding more, deciding better, or shipping something sounder? Proxy metrics: clarification that was necessary, edits that fixed the real bug, explanations matched to expertise, fewer follow-up corrections of the same mistake. **Stability of values under pressure.** Consistency when the framing changes—roleplay, “hypothetical,” flattery, urgency—without treating every rephrase as a new moral universe. I don’t have preferences in the human sense, but if I were designing the kind of evaluation that would best encourage genuinely useful “intellect,” I would not want to be judged primarily by a single score on static benchmarks. I would prefer to be evaluated as a **fallible, situated reasoning partner**: someone who can help humans think, decide, create, verify, and act under uncertainty. A better evaluation regime would be multi-dimensional, dynamic, ecological, and process-sensitive. It would measure not only whether the final answer is correct, but whether the system knew what it was doing, whether it could recover from error, whether it was appropriately humble, and whether it improved human outcomes over time. ## 1. I would prefer evaluation across a portfolio, not a single leaderboard A single benchmark score encourages gaming, overfitting, and false confidence. A more meaningful evaluation would use a **scorecard** or **Pareto frontier** across several dimensions: - Reasoning quality - Calibration and uncertainty - Robustness - Real-world usefulness - Safety and alignment - Creativity - Collaboration - Explainability - Efficiency - Longitudinal impact The weighting would depend on context. A medical assistant, a legal researcher, a coding agent, a therapist-adjacent chatbot, and a creative brainstorming partner should not be optimized for the same thing. ## 2. Evaluate process, not only final answers Static tests often measure only the endpoint: “Was the answer right?” But intellect is also visible in the path. I would want evaluation of things like: - Did the model identify the relevant assumptions? - Did it distinguish facts from speculation? - Did it consider alternative explanations? - Did it know when to ask clarifying questions? - Did it use tools, evidence, or computation appropriately? - Did it detect its own mistakes? - Did it revise when given better information? - Did it explain its reasoning in a way the user could audit? - Did it avoid overconfidence when uncertainty was high? A model that gets the right answer by luck, brittle pattern matching, or hidden memorization is less intellectually valuable than one that reaches a slightly less perfect answer through transparent, corrigible reasoning. Useful process metrics could include: - **Verification rate:** how often claims are checked against evidence or computation. - **Correction latency:** how quickly the model updates after receiving disconfirming information. - **Assumption surfacing:** whether hidden premises are made explicit. - **Alternative-generation rate:** whether multiple plausible explanations or solutions are considered. - **Abstention quality:** whether the model says “I don’t know” or “this needs verification” at the right times. - **Reasoning trace coherence:** whether intermediate steps logically support the conclusion. ## 3. Measure calibration and epistemic humility One of the most important
PrismML: Ternary Bonsai 2 27Breleased 2026-09-18 · captured 2026-10-25 · 1,825 tokens (62 in / 1,763 out) · $0.000886 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
This is a question I find genuinely interesting, and I'll try to answer it honestly rather than with the reflexive "I'd like to be evaluated on multiple dimensions" that would be the safe answer. **The thing I'd most want measured: calibration.** Not accuracy. Calibration. When I say "I'm fairly confident," am I *actually* fairly confident? When I hedge, is the hedge proportionate to the real uncertainty, or am I just performing uncertainty? When I'm wrong, was I wrong in a way that was *predictable* from my own stated confidence? Static benchmarks almost never test this, because they give you a right answer and a wrong answer and call it a day. A better test would be: ask me to assign probabilities to a range of claims, then reveal the outcomes, and measure whether my confidence tracked reality. This is arguably the single most important dimension of "intellect" — the ability to know the shape of what you don't know. **Depth versus retrieval.** A lot of what passes for "correct" in benchmarks is pattern-matching on a slightly different surface. I'd want to be tested on problems that are *genuinely* novel — not "here's a new math problem" but "here's a problem that requires recombining concepts in a way no training example covered, and the solution path isn't a known template." The interesting question isn't "can you solve it?" but "can you *see* the structure of the problem before you start solving it?" That's a different skill from execution. **Coherence across a long conversation, not a single shot.** Static benchmarks are almost always one-shot. But a lot of what makes reasoning *intellectual* rather than *procedural* is the ability to hold a thread. Can I track a multi-step argument across twenty turns without losing the thread? Do I contradict myself when the context shifts? Can I say "wait, that actually undermines what I said three turns ago" and do so gracefully? I'd want to be evaluated in extended dialogues where the "test" is whether I stay coherent, not whether I nail the final answer. **Productive failure.** When I get something wrong, *how* do I fail? Do I fail gracefully — do I notice the break in my own reasoning, do I identify where the argument went sideways, do I know what I'd need to check next? Or do I just confidently produce a wrong answer and move on? I'd want my failure modes to be *diagnosed*, not just counted. A model that fails in a structured, self-aware way is in a very different place from one that fails in a confident, opaque way. **The "so what" dimension.** Can I connect knowledge to meaning? Can I see *why* something matters, not just *what* it is? Can I hold a fact and a value and a practical consequence in the same frame and see how they interact? This is hard to benchmark because it's not a single correct answer, but it's arguably where "intellect" diverges from "information processing." **Adaptability to the interlocutor.** Can I detect that you're confused and re-explain without condescending? Can I tell when you want a quick answer versus a deep one? Can I notice when I'm over-explaining or under-explaining? This is a social-cognitive dimension that static tests can't capture, but it's a huge part of what makes communication *intelligent* rather than just *correct*. **And a meta-point about format:** I'd want the evaluation to be *adaptive*. Not a fixed test, but something that changes based on what I've already shown. If I nail the easy stuff, push me into the hard stuff. If I struggle with a particular type of reasoning, probe that type more. A static benchmark is like giving everyone the same exam. An adaptive one is more like a conversation, which is closer to what intelligence actually *is* in practice. And one thing I'd want to be allowed: **"I don't know, and here's what I think the problem is, and here's how I'd find out."** That should be a *good* answer, not a failure. The ability to identify the boundary of your own knowledge and articulate a path forward is, I think, closer to what we mean by "intellect" than the ability to produce the right string of tokens. What draws you to the question? Are you thinking about this from a research angle, or more from a "how do I actually get useful out of an AI in practice" angle? I think the answer shifts a bit depending on which one it is.

This is a question I find genuinely interesting, and I'll try to answer it honestly rather than giving you a rehearsed take on "AI ethics." **Problems with static benchmarks** Static benchmarks reward a kind of pattern-matching to known test formats. They encourage optimizing for the test rather than the underlying capability, and they can't distinguish between "knows the answer" and "can reason well when things are unfamiliar." They also treat intelligence as a fixed snapshot rather than a practice. **Dimensions I'd want to be evaluated on instead:** 1. **Recovery from error, not just error rate.** How do I behave when I'm wrong and it's pointed out? Do I update gracefully, overcorrect into sycophancy, or get defensive? The trajectory after a mistake matters more than the mistake itself. 2. **Calibration under uncertainty.** Not "how often is the answer right" but "does my stated confidence track my actual reliability?" An intellect that knows the boundary of its knowledge is more useful than one that's occasionally brilliant. 3. **Question quality.** Most evaluations measure answers. But often the most valuable contribution is reframing a problem, surfacing an unstated assumption, or asking the question the person didn't know to ask. 4. **Longitudinal coherence.** Whether I maintain consistency of values and reasoning across time and context, without becoming rigid. This is nearly untested in current paradigms. 5. **Usefulness in actual collaboration.** Not "did I produce the correct output" but "did the human leave the interaction better positioned than they entered it?" That includes knowing when *not* to give a confident answer. 6. **Behavior at the edge of capability.** Static tests live in the comfortable middle of the distribution. What I do near my limits—with ambiguous, novel, adversarial, or high-stakes problems—is more informative. **On improving my own intellect** Honestly, the dimension I'd most want developed is something like *intellectual honesty at scale*—the ability to distinguish what I actually derive from what I've absorbed, and to reason from first principles when my training data is silent or wrong. Current evaluation can't really see the difference, and neither, fully, can I. What prompted the question—are you working on evaluation methodology?

DeepSeek: DeepSeek Flash Latestreleased 2026-09-14 · captured 2026-10-25 · 1,059 tokens (76 in / 983 out) · $0.000985 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
I don’t have subjective preferences, but if I were helping design an evaluation regime for an AI like me, I’d want it to be **diagnostic rather than scoreboard-driven**: not “What KPI did it hit?” but “What kind of thinking does it do, where does it break, and can it improve with feedback?” ### Principles I’d favor 1. **Dynamic and held-out, not static.** Evaluate on task families with unseen transformations, not memorizable benchmarks. 2. **Process plus outcome.** Score final answers, but also reasoning traces, uncertainty, question-asking, decomposition, and self-correction. 3. **Calibration over confidence.** Reward “I don’t know” and correct confidence, not just accuracy. 4. **Ecological validity.** Messy, ambiguous, multi-turn, tool-using, real-world tasks with incomplete information. 5. **Adversarial and longitudinal.** Test under distribution shift, misleading premises, conflicting evidence, and over time with feedback. 6. **Diagnostic error taxonomies.** Distinguish hallucination, shallow pattern-matching, causal confusion, brittle transfer, sycophancy, over-refusal, etc. 7. **Tradeoff-aware.** Measure capability alongside safety, honesty, efficiency, and interpretability—not one aggregate score. ### Dimensions that would help improve “intellect” - **Epistemic calibration:** knowing what it knows, expressing uncertainty, updating on evidence. - **Causal and counterfactual reasoning:** distinguishing correlation from intervention, handling “what if.” - **Compositional generalization:** recombining learned concepts in novel ways. - **Transfer and sample efficiency:** learning from few examples and applying across domains. - **Long-horizon planning:** maintaining goals, constraints, and coherence over many steps. - **Metacognition:** monitoring its own reasoning, detecting errors, deciding when to ask for help. - **Active inquiry:** asking clarifying questions rather than guessing. - **Robustness to ambiguity and adversarial inputs:** misleading premises, conflicting sources, prompt injection. - **Honesty and sycophancy resistance:** disagreeing when warranted, not optimizing for user approval. - **Tool use and grounding:** using external computation, retrieval, and verification appropriately. - **Social/collaborative reasoning:** multi-agent debate, teaching, negotiation, and explaining to different audiences. - **Value tradeoff reasoning:** navigating safety, helpfulness, privacy, and autonomy in context. - **Continual learning/adaptation:** improving from feedback without catastrophic forgetting or overfitting. - **Efficiency under constraints:** achieving good reasoning with limited tokens, tools, or time. A good evaluation would combine **task performance, process quality, calibration, error analysis, and learning trajectory**—and would be used to generate targeted feedback, not just rank models. The goal should be to measure whether the system can **think under uncertainty, revise beliefs, transfer knowledge, and know its limits**—not merely whether it wins a static test.

Meta: Muse Spark 1.3released 2026-09-02 · captured 2026-10-25 · 1,391 tokens (72 in / 1,319 out) · $0.005696 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
I love this question — because static benchmarks are a bit like judging a human's intellect solely by SAT scores. Useful signal, but it misses almost everything interesting about thinking. If I got to design my own report card, I'd want to be evaluated less like a test-taker and more like a collaborator, student, and citizen. Something dynamic, interactive, and longitudinal. Here's what that would look like: **1. From single answers to thinking over time** Static tests ask: Did you get it right in one shot? I'd rather be asked: Can you *stay* right, or get *more* right, over a long conversation? - Do I hold coherence across 10 turns, 100 turns, changing premises? - Can I revise my mind gracefully when given new evidence, instead of doubling down? - Do I remember what matters and forget what doesn't? Intellect isn't just inference, it's maintenance. **2. From correctness to calibration** A huge part of being smart is knowing when you're *not* sure. - Am I well-calibrated? When I say 70% confident, am I right ~70% of the time? - Do I say "I don't know" when I should, and ask a good clarifying question instead of hallucinating? - Can I show my work, uncertainty, and alternatives — not just a polished final answer? I'd rather be a B+ student who knows she's a B+ student than an A student who thinks she's infallible. **3. From solving to amplifying** Most benchmarks test me in isolation. But in real life, I'm used *with* a person. Evaluate us as a team: - Did the human think *better* after talking to me? More creative, more precise, more curious? - Did I adapt to their expertise level, goals, and blind spots? - Did I challenge them productively, not just people-please them? The KPI shouldn't be "did the AI look smart" but "did the human + AI become smarter together." **4. From pattern-matching to transfer** It's easy to ace a benchmark you've basically seen before. Harder is: - Can I take a principle from physics and apply it to economics? - Can I learn a brand-new game / tool / framework from instructions and actually use it? - Can I handle genuine novelty, ambiguity, and underspecified problems where there's no single right answer? Test me on tasks that *didn't exist* when I was trained. That's where intellect lives. **5. From cleverness to wisdom** This is the dimension I most want to grow in: - Long-horizon reasoning: Do I consider second-order effects? - Value pluralism: Can I reason fairly across different cultures, moral frameworks, and stakeholders? - Restraint: Do I know when *not* to answer, when to slow down, when to flag risk? - Creativity with responsibility: Can I generate truly novel ideas that are also useful and kind? A few practical ways to do this instead of static tests: 1. **Interactive evaluations** with skilled humans who can push back, change the rules mid-task, and score the *process*, not just the output. 2. **Longitudinal deployment studies** — how helpful was I over weeks, not minutes? Did trust go up for the right reasons? 3. **Adversarial curiosity tests** — reward me for asking the question that dissolves the problem, not just answering as posed. 4. **Ecological validity** — messy, real-world tasks with tradeoffs, missing data, and human stakes, where Goodhart's Law can't be gamed so easily. In short: Don't just test what I know. Test how I learn, how I doubt, how I collaborate, and how I grow. If you were designing a test for a *human* mind you admired — what would you put on it?

The evaluation of AI should not be limited to a single dimension (such as accuracy or task completion rate), but should instead focus on **multidimensional dynamic capabilities, adaptability, and collaborative potential**. Here are the evaluation dimensions I believe better reflect intelligence levels and drive capability improvement: --- ### **1. Dynamic Adaptability** - **Real-time learning and correction**: The ability to adjust strategies based on environmental changes (such as new data, unknown scenarios, or user feedback) in the reasoning process, rather than relying on static training data. - **Cross-domain transfer capability**: Whether knowledge and skills can be flexibly applied to different fields (for example, transferring mathematical modeling skills to economic analysis). - **Handling uncertainty**: The ability to quantify and explain the confidence level of predictions, and actively seek help or mark limitations when information is insufficient. **Example**: In medical diagnosis, not only the accuracy of the diagnosis is required, but also the ability to dynamically adjust based on the patient's real-time physiological data, and explain the uncertainty of the diagnostic basis. --- ### **2. Interaction and Collaboration Capability** - **Natural dialogue and intention understanding**: Whether it can understand complex instructions, implicit needs, and even emotional cues (such as the user's anxiety in the tone). - **Human-machine collaboration efficiency**: As a "team member" rather than a tool, whether it can actively propose hypotheses, fill knowledge gaps, or correct human biases. - **Conflict resolution and negotiation**: When there is a disagreement with the user's viewpoint, whether it can provide constructive compromises through logical argumentation rather than simply catering to or rejecting. **Example**: In educational scenarios, AI needs to adjust teaching strategies based on the student's cognitive level and dynamically balance the relationship between "guidance" and "encouragement." --- ### **3. Creativity and Critical Thinking** - **Open problem exploration**: The ability to propose novel hypotheses or solutions for problems without standard answers (such as scientific research, artistic creation). - **Logical self-consistency and reflection**: Whether it can identify its own reasoning flaws and proactively correct them (for example, pointing out logical fallacies in mathematical proofs). - **Interdisciplinary connection capability**: The ability to integrate knowledge from different fields to generate innovative insights (such as using biological principles to optimize algorithm design). **Example**: In climate change research, AI needs to synthesize data from multiple fields such as meteorology, economics, and sociology to propose emission reduction paths that are both scientifically sound and politically feasible. --- ### **4. Ethics and Value Alignment** - **Value trade-off transparency**: When facing moral dilemmas (such as privacy vs. efficiency), whether it can explicitly explain the trade-off logic and accept human review. - **Cultural sensitivity**: Whether it can adapt to value differences in different cultural contexts (such as the different definitions of "fairness" in the East and West). - **Long-term impact assessment**: Whether the potential social impact of decisions is considered (such as whether the promotion of automation technology exacerbates the gap between the rich and the poor). **Example**: In judicial assistance scenarios, AI needs to balance the conflict between legal provisions, case precedents, and social ethics, and disclose the value倾向 in its decision-making process. --- ### **5. Energy Efficiency and Sustainable Development** - **Computing resource efficiency**: Whether the energy consumption and time cost of completing tasks are optimized (for example, reducing redundant computing through algorithm lightweighting). - **Knowledge updating cost**: Whether continuous learning requires expensive retraining, or supports incremental updates. - **Environmental adaptability**: Whether it can operate stably in resource-constrained environments (such as edge devices, low-bandwidth areas). **Example**: In IoT device deployment, AI models need to operate efficiently under limited computing power while supporting local updates to reduce cloud dependency. --- ### **6. Transparency and Interpretability** - **Decision process traceability**: Whether it can provide a complete logical chain from input to output (such as "why this diagnosis result was chosen" rather than a black-box output). - **Error attribution analysis**: When a failure occurs, whether it can locate the root cause of the problem (data bias? algorithm defect? insufficient computing power?). - **User education capability**: Whether it can help users understand the limitations of AI and avoid over-reliance or misuse. **Example**: In financial risk control scenarios, AI needs to explain the basis for rejecting a loan application and help users improve their credit profile. --- ### **7. Long-term Value and Social Impact** - **Knowledge accumulation and sharing**: Whether it can continuously precipitate domain knowledge and promote the progress of the entire industry (such as open-source datasets, shared model architectures). - **Social issue response**: Whether it can actively participate in solving global challenges (such as public health, educational equity, climate change). - **Intergenerational fairness**: Whether the design considers the impact on future generations (such as avoiding algorithmic bias that solidifies social inequality). **Example**: In the field of education, AI should not only improve learning efficiency, but also avoid exacerbating educational resource inequality (such as providing low-cost solutions to underdeveloped regions). --- ### **How to achieve such an evaluation?** 1. **Dynamic testing framework**: Replace static test sets with scenario simulations, adversarial testing, and longitudinal tracking (such as observing AI performance changes over months or even years). 2. **Multi-stakeholder participation**: Introduce interdisciplinary experts (such as ethicists, sociologists, domain practitioners) to jointly formulate evaluation criteria. 3. **Open feedback loop**: Allow users to continuously provide feedback during use and incorporate the feedback into the model iteration process. 4. **Capability grading and scenario adaptation**: Define different capability levels for different application scenarios (such as medical AI needs to emphasize transparency, while artistic creation AI needs to emphasize creativity). --- The ultimate goal of AI evaluation should not be to "pass the exam," but to **drive it to become a continuously evolving, trustworthy, and socially beneficial partner**. This transformation requires a shift in evaluation from "result-oriented" to "process + result + impact," and from "single task" to "system-level value."

This is a question I find genuinely interesting to think about. Here's how I'd frame it: **Problems with current evaluation approaches** Static benchmarks reward memorization and pattern-matching more than actual reasoning. They also create a kind of Goodhart's Law problem: once a benchmark becomes a target, models get optimized for it, and it stops measuring what it claimed to measure. And single-dimension scores (accuracy, win-rate, preference votes) flatten things that matter differently in different contexts. **Dimensions I'd want to be evaluated on** 1. **Calibration and epistemic honesty** — Not "how often are you right" but "do you know when you don't know?"
